<span class="calibre4">\
</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 1. Not just that every day more of our life is used up and less and less of it is left, but this too: if we live longer, can we be sure our mind will still be up to understanding the world—to the contemplation that aims at divine and human knowledge? If our mind starts to wander, we’ll still go on breathing, go on eating, imagining things, feeling urges and so on. But getting the most out of ourselves, calculating where our duty lies, analyzing what we hear and see, deciding whether it’s time to call it quits—all the things you need a healthy mind for . . . all those are gone.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> So we need to hurry.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> Not just because we move daily closer to death but also because our understanding—our grasp of the world—may be gone before we get there.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 2. We should remember that even Nature’s inadvertence has its own charm, its own attractiveness. The way loaves of bread split open on top in the oven; the ridges are just by-products of the baking, and yet pleasing, somehow: they rouse our appetite without our knowing why.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> Or how ripe figs begin to burst.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> And olives on the point of falling: the shadow of decay gives them a peculiar beauty.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> Stalks of wheat bending under their own weight. The furrowed brow of the lion. Flecks of foam on the boar’s mouth.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> And other things. If you look at them in isolation there’s nothing beautiful about them, and yet by supplementing nature they enrich it and draw us in. And anyone with a feeling for nature—a deeper sensitivity—will find it all gives pleasure. Even what seems inadvertent. He’ll find the jaws of live animals as beautiful as painted ones or sculptures. He’ll look calmly at the distinct beauty of old age in men, women, and at the loveliness of children. And other things like that will call out to him constantly—things unnoticed by others. Things seen only by those at home with Nature and its works.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 3. Hippocrates cured many illnesses—and then fell ill and died. The Chaldaeans predicted the deaths of many others; in due course their own hour arrived. Alexander, Pompey, Caesar—who utterly destroyed so many cities, cut down so many thousand foot and horse in battle—they too departed this life. Heraclitus often told us the world would end in fire. But it was moisture that carried him off; he died smeared with cowshit. Democritus was killed by ordinary vermin, Socrates by the human kind.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> And?</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> You boarded, you set sail, you’ve made the passage. Time to disembark. If it’s for another life, well, there’s nowhere without gods on that side either. If to nothingness, then you no longer have to put up with pain and pleasure, or go on dancing attendance on this battered crate, your body—so much inferior to that which serves it.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> One is mind and spirit, the other earth and garbage.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 4. Don’t waste the rest of your time here worrying about other people—unless it affects the common good. It will keep you from doing anything useful. You’ll be too preoccupied with what so-and-so is doing, and why, and what they’re saying, and what they’re thinking, and what they’re up to, and all the other things that throw you off and keep you from focusing on your own mind.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> You need to avoid certain things in your train of thought: everything random, everything irrelevant. And certainly everything self-important or malicious. You need to get used to winnowing your thoughts, so that if someone says, “What are you thinking about?” you can respond at once (and truthfully) that you are thinking this or thinking that. And it would be obvious at once from your answer that your thoughts were straightforward and considerate ones—the thoughts of an unselfish person, one unconcerned with pleasure and with sensual indulgence generally, with squabbling, with slander and envy, or anything else you’d be ashamed to be caught thinking.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> Someone like that—someone who refuses to put off joining the elect—is a kind of priest, a servant of the gods, in touch with what is within him and what keeps a person undefiled by pleasures, invulnerable to any pain, untouched by arrogance, unaffected by meanness, an athlete in the greatest of all contests—the struggle not to be overwhelmed by anything that happens. With what leaves us dyed indelibly by justice, welcoming wholeheartedly whatever comes—whatever we’re assigned—not worrying too often, or with any selfish motive, about what other people say. Or do, or think.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> He does only what is his to do, and considers constantly what the world has in store for him—doing his best, and trusting that all is *for* the best. For we carry our fate with us—and it carries us.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> He keeps in mind that all rational things are related, and that to care for all human beings is part of being human. Which doesn’t mean we have to share their opinions. We should listen only to those whose lives conform to nature. And the others? He bears in mind what sort of people they are—both at home and abroad, by night as well as day—and who they spend their time with. And he cares nothing for their praise—men who can’t even meet their own standards.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 5. How to act:</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre4">\
</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre4"> Never under compulsion, out of selfishness, without forethought, with misgivings.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre4"> Don’t gussy up your thoughts.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre4"> No surplus words or unnecessary actions.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre4"> Let the spirit in you represent a man, an adult, a citizen, a Roman, a ruler. Taking up his post like a soldier and patiently awaiting his recall from life. Needing no oath or witness.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre4"> Cheerfulness. Without requiring other people’s help. Or serenity supplied by others.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre4"> To stand up straight—not straightened.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 6. If, at some point in your life, you should come across anything better than justice, honesty, self-control, courage—than a mind satisfied that it has succeeded in enabling you to act rationally, and satisfied to accept what’s beyond its control—if you find anything better than that, embrace it without reservations—it must be an extraordinary thing indeed—and enjoy it to the full.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> But if nothing presents itself that’s superior to the spirit that lives within—the one that has subordinated individual desires to itself, that discriminates among impressions, that has broken free of physical temptations (as Socrates used to say), and subordinated itself to the gods, and looks out for human beings’ welfare—if you find that there’s nothing more important or valuable than that . . .</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> . . . then don’t make room for anything but it—for anything that might lead you astray, tempt you off the road, and leave you unable to devote yourself completely to achieving the goodness that is uniquely yours. It would be wrong for anything to stand between you and attaining goodness—as a rational being and a citizen. Anything at all: the applause of the crowd, high office, wealth, or self-indulgence. All of them might seem to be compatible with it—for a while. But suddenly they control us and sweep us away.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> So make your choice straightforwardly, once and for all, and stick to it. Choose what’s best. </span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> —Best is what benefits *me*.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> As a rational being? Then follow through. Or just as an animal? Then say so and stand your ground without making a show of it. (Just make sure you’ve done your homework first.)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 7. Never regard something as doing you good if it makes you betray a trust, or lose your sense of shame, or makes you show hatred, suspicion, ill will, or hypocrisy, or a desire for things best done behind closed doors. If you can privilege your own mind, your guiding spirit and your reverence for its powers, that should keep you clear of dramatics, of wailing and gnashing of teeth. You won’t need solitude—or a cast of thousands, either. Above all, you’ll be free of fear and desire. And how long your body will contain the soul that inhabits it will cause you not a moment’s worry. If it’s time for you to go, leave willingly—as you would to accomplish anything that can be done with grace and honor. And concentrate on this, your whole life long: for your mind to be in the right state—the state a rational, civic mind should be in.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 8. The mind of one set straight and purified: no pus, no dirt, no scabs.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> And not a life cut short by death, like an actor who stops before the play is done, the plot wound up.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> Neither servility nor arrogance. Neither cringing nor disdain. Neither excuses nor evasions.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 9. Your ability to control your thoughts—treat it with respect. It’s all that protects your mind from false perceptions—false to your nature, and that of all rational beings. It’s what makes thoughtfulness possible, and affection for other people, and submission to the divine.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 10. Forget everything else. Keep hold of this alone and remember it: Each of us lives only now, this brief instant. The rest has been lived already, or is impossible to see. The span we live is small—small as the corner of the earth in which we live it. Small as even the greatest renown, passed from mouth to mouth by short-lived stick figures, ignorant alike of themselves and those long dead.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 11. To the stand-bys above, add this one: always to define whatever it is we perceive—to trace its outline—so we can see what it really is: its substance. Stripped bare. As a whole. Unmodified. And to call it by its name—the thing itself and its components, to which it will eventually return. Nothing is so conducive to spiritual growth as this capacity for logical and accurate analysis of everything that happens to us. To look at it in such a way that we understand what need it fulfills, and in what kind of world. And its value to that world as a whole and to man in particular—as a citizen of that higher city, of which all other cities are mere households.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> What is it—this thing that now forces itself on my notice? What is it made up of? How long was it designed to last? And what qualities do I need to bring to bear on it—tranquillity, courage, honesty, trustworthiness, straightforwardness, independence or what?</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> So in each case you need to say: “This is due to God.” Or: “This is due to the interweavings and intertwinings of fate, to coincidence or chance.” Or: “This is due to a human being. Someone of the same race, the same birth, the same society, but who doesn’t know what nature requires of him. But I do. And so I’ll treat them as the law that binds us—the law of nature—requires. With kindness and with justice.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> And in inconsequential things? I’ll do my best to treat them as they deserve.”</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 12. If you do the job in a principled way, with diligence, energy and patience, if you keep yourself free of distractions, and keep the spirit inside you undamaged, as if you might have to give it back at any moment—</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> If you can embrace this without fear or expectation—can find fulfillment in what you’re doing now, as Nature intended, and in superhuman truthfulness (every word, every utterance)—then your life will be happy.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> No one can prevent that.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 13. Doctors keep their scalpels and other instruments handy, for emergencies. Keep your philosophy ready too—ready to understand heaven and earth. In everything you do, even the smallest thing, remember the chain that links them. Nothing earthly succeeds by ignoring heaven, nothing heavenly by ignoring the earth.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 14. Stop drifting. You’re not going to re-read your *Brief Comments,* your *Deeds of the Ancient Greeks and Romans,* the commonplace books you saved for your old age. Sprint for the finish. Write off your hopes, and if your well-being matters to you, be your own savior while you can.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 15. They don’t realize how much is included in *stealing, sowing, buying, resting, seeing to business* (not with the eyes, but another kind of sight).</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 16. Body. Soul. Mind.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> Sensations: the body.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> Desires: the soul.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> Reasoning: the mind.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> To experience sensations: even grazing beasts do that. To let your desires control you: even wild animals do that—and rutting humans, and tyrants (from Phalaris to Nero . . .).</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> To make your mind your guide to what seems best: even people who deny the gods do that. Even people who betray their country. Even people who do \<. . .\> behind closed doors.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> If all the rest is common coin, then what is unique to the good man?</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> To welcome with affection what is sent by fate. Not to stain or disturb the spirit within him with a mess of false beliefs. Instead, to preserve it faithfully, by calmly obeying God—saying nothing untrue, doing nothing unjust. And if the others don’t acknowledge it—this life lived with simplicity, humility, cheerfulness—he doesn’t resent them for it, and isn’t deterred from following the road where it leads: to the end of life. An end to be approached in purity, in serenity, in acceptance, in peaceful unity with what must be.</span>

</div>

<div id="index_split_012.html_filepos178805" style="height:0pt">

<div class="calibre_4">

<div class="calibre_2">

<span class="calibre3"> <span class="small">B</span>ook <span class="small">4</span></span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_3">

 

</div>

<span id="index_split_013.html"></span>

 

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4">\
</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4">\
</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4">\
</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 1. Our inward power, when it obeys nature, reacts to events by accommodating itself to what it faces—to what is possible. It needs no specific material. It pursues its own aims as circumstances allow; it turns obstacles into fuel. As a fire overwhelms what would have quenched a lamp. What’s thrown on top of the conflagration is absorbed, consumed by it—and makes it burn still higher.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 2. No random actions, none not based on underlying principles.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 3. People try to get away from it all—to the country, to the beach, to the mountains. You always wish that you could too. Which is idiotic: you can get away from it anytime you like.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> By going within.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> Nowhere you can go is more peaceful—more free of interruptions—than your own soul. Especially if you have other things to rely on. An instant’s recollection and there it is: complete tranquillity. And by tranquillity I mean a kind of harmony.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> So keep getting away from it all—like that. Renew yourself. But keep it brief and basic. A quick visit should be enough to ward off all \< . . . \> and send you back ready to face what awaits you.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> What’s there to complain about? People’s misbehavior? But take into consideration:</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_13">

<span class="calibre4">\
</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_13">

<span class="calibre4"> • that rational beings exist for one another;</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_13">

<span class="calibre4"> • that doing what’s right sometimes requires patience;</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_13">

<span class="calibre4"> • that no one does the wrong thing deliberately;</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_13">

<span class="calibre4"> • and the number of people who have feuded and envied and hated and fought and died and been buried.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_13">

<span class="calibre4">\
</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> . . . and keep your mouth shut.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> Or are you complaining about the things the world assigns you? But consider the two options: Providence or atoms. And all the arguments for seeing the world as a city.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> Or is it your body? Keep in mind that when the mind detaches itself and realizes its own nature, it no longer has anything to do with ordinary life—the rough and the smooth, either one. And remember all you’ve been taught—and accepted—about pain and pleasure.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> Or is it your reputation that’s bothering you? But look at how soon we’re all forgotten. The abyss of endless time that swallows it all. The emptiness of all those applauding hands. The people who praise us—how capricious they are, how arbitrary. And the tiny region in which it all takes place. The whole earth a point in space—and most of it uninhabited. How many people there will be to admire you, and who they are.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> No. It’s fortunate that this has happened and I’ve remained unharmed by it—not shattered by the present or frightened of the future. It could have happened to anyone. But not everyone could have remained unharmed by it. Why treat the one as a misfortune rather than the other as fortunate? Can you really call something a misfortune that doesn’t violate human nature? Or do you think something that’s not against nature’s will can violate it? But you know what its will is. Does what’s happened keep you from acting with justice, generosity, self-control, sanity, prudence, honesty, humility, straightforwardness, and all the other qualities that allow a person’s nature to fulfill itself?</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> So remember this principle when something threatens to cause you pain: the thing itself was no misfortune at all; to endure it and prevail is great good fortune.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 51. Take the shortest route, the one that nature planned—to speak and act in the healthiest way. Do that, and be free of pain and stress, free of all calculation and pretension.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 1. At dawn, when you have trouble getting out of bed, tell yourself: “I have to go to work—as a human being. What do I have to complain of, if I’m going to do what I was born for—the things I was brought into the world to do? Or is *this* what I was created for? To huddle under the blankets and stay warm?</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> —But it’s nicer here. . . .</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> So you were born to feel “nice”? Instead of doing things and experiencing them? Don’t you see the plants, the birds, the ants and spiders and bees going about their individual tasks, putting the world in order, as best they can? And you’re not willing to do your job as a human being? Why aren’t you running to do what your nature demands?</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> —But we have to sleep sometime. . . .</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> Agreed. But nature set a limit on that—as it did on eating and drinking. And you’re over the limit. You’ve had more than enough of that. But not of working. There you’re still below your quota.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> You don’t love yourself enough. Or you’d love your nature too, and what it demands of you. People who love what they do wear themselves down doing it, they even forget to wash or eat. Do you have less respect for your own nature than the engraver does for engraving, the dancer for the dance, the miser for money or the social climber for status? When they’re really possessed by what they do, they’d rather stop eating and sleeping than give up practicing their arts.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> Is helping others less valuable to you? Not worth your effort?</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 2.To shrug it all off and wipe it clean—every annoyance and distraction—and reach utter stillness.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> Child’s play.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 3. If an action or utterance is appropriate, then it’s appropriate for you. Don’t be put off by other people’s comments and criticism. If it’s right to say or do it, then it’s the right thing for you to do or say.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> The others obey their own lead, follow their own impulses. Don’t be distracted. Keep walking. Follow your own nature, and follow Nature—along the road they share.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 4. I walk through what is natural, until the time comes to sink down and rest. To entrust my last breath to the source of my daily breathing, fall on the source of my father’s seed, of my mother’s blood, of my nurse’s milk. Of my daily food and drink through all these years. What sustains my footsteps, and the use I make of it—the many uses.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 5. No one could ever accuse you of being quick-witted.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> All right, but there are plenty of other things you can’t claim you “haven’t got in you.” Practice the virtues you *can* show: honesty, gravity, endurance, austerity, resignation, abstinence, patience, sincerity, moderation, seriousness, high-mindedness. Don’t you see how much you have to offer—beyond excuses like “can’t”? And yet you still settle for less.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> Or is it some inborn condition that makes you whiny and grasping and obsequious, makes you complain about your body and curry favor and show off and leaves you so turbulent inside?</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> No. You could have broken free a long way back. And then you would have been only a little slow. “Not so quick on the uptake.”</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> And you need to work on that as well—that slowness. Not something to be ignored, let alone to prize.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 6. Some people, when they do someone a favor, are always looking for a chance to call it in. And some aren’t, but they’re still aware of it—still regard it as a debt. But others don’t even do that. They’re like a vine that produces grapes without looking for anything in return.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> A horse at the end of the race . . .</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> A dog when the hunt is over . . .</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> A bee with its honey stored . . .</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> And a human being after helping others.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> They don’t make a fuss about it. They just go on to something else, as the vine looks forward to bearing fruit again in season.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> We should be like that. Acting almost unconsciously.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> —Yes. Except conscious of it. Because it’s characteristic of social beings that they see themselves as acting socially. And expect their neighbors to see it too!</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> That’s true. But you’re misunderstanding me. You’ll wind up like the people I mentioned before, misled by plausible reasoning. But if you make an effort to understand what I’m saying, then you won’t need to worry about neglecting your social duty.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 8. Just as you overhear people saying that “the doctor prescribed such-and-such for him” (like riding, or cold baths, or walking barefoot . . .), say this: “Nature prescribed illness for him.” Or blindness. Or the loss of a limb. Or whatever. There “prescribed” means something like “ordered, so as to further his recovery.” And so too here. What happens to each of us is ordered. It furthers our destiny.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> And when we describe things as “taking place,” we’re talking like builders, who say that blocks in a wall or a pyramid “take their place” in the structure, and fit together in a harmonious pattern.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> For there is a single harmony. Just as the world forms a single body comprising all bodies, so fate forms a single purpose, comprising all purposes. Even complete illiterates acknowledge it when they say that something “brought on” this or that. Brought on, yes. Or prescribed it. And in that case, let’s accept it—as we accept what the doctor prescribes. It may not always be pleasant, but we embrace it—because we want to get well. Look at the accomplishment of nature’s plans in that light—the way you look at your own health—and accept what happens (even if it seems hard to accept). Accept it because of what it leads to: the good health of the world, and the well-being and prosperity of Zeus himself, who would not have brought this on anyone unless it brought benefit to the world as a whole. No nature would do that—bring something about that wasn’t beneficial to what it governed.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> So there are two reasons to embrace what happens. One is that it’s happening to *you.* It was prescribed for you, and it pertains to you. The thread was spun long ago, by the oldest cause of all.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> The other reason is that what happens to an individual is a cause of well-being in what directs the world—of its well-being, its fulfillment, of its very existence, even. Because the whole is damaged if you cut away anything—anything at all—from its continuity and its coherence. Not only its parts, but its purposes. And that’s what you’re doing when you complain: hacking and destroying.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 9. Not to feel exasperated, or defeated, or despondent because your days aren’t packed with wise and moral actions. But to get back up when you fail, to celebrate behaving like a human—however imperfectly—and fully embrace the pursuit that you’ve embarked on.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> And not to think of philosophy as your instructor, but as the sponge and egg white that relieve ophthalmia—as a soothing ointment, a warm lotion. Not showing off your obedience to the *logos,* but resting in it. Remember: philosophy requires only what your nature already demands. What you’ve been after is something else again—something unnatural.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> —But what could be preferable?</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> That’s exactly how pleasure traps us, isn’t it? Wouldn’t magnanimity be preferable? Or freedom? Honesty? Prudence? Piety? And is there anything preferable to thought itself—to logic, to understanding? Think of their surefootedness. Their fluent stillness.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 10. Things are wrapped in such a veil of mystery that many good philosophers have found it impossible to make sense of them. Even the Stoics have trouble. Any assessment we make is subject to alteration—just as we are ourselves.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> Look closely at them—how impermanent they are, how meaningless. Things that a pervert can own, a whore, a thief.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> Then look at the way the people around you behave. Even the best of them are hard to put up with—not to mention putting up with yourself. In such deep darkness, such a sewer—in the flux of material, of time, of motion and things moved—I don’t know what there is to value or to work for.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> Quite the contrary. We need to comfort ourselves and wait for dissolution. And not get impatient in the meantime, but take refuge in these two things:</span>

<span class="calibre4"> i. Nothing can happen to me that isn’t natural.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> ii. I can keep from doing anything that God and my own spirit don’t approve. No one can force me to.</span>

<span class="calibre4"> 11. What am I doing with my soul?</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> Interrogate yourself, to find out what inhabits your so-called mind and what kind of soul you have now. A child’s soul, an adolescent’s, a woman’s? A tyrant’s soul? The soul of a predator—or its prey?</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 12. Another way to grasp what ordinary people mean by “goods”:</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> Suppose you took certain things as touchstones of goodness: prudence, self-control, justice, and courage, say. If you understood “goods” as meaning those, you wouldn’t be able to follow that line about “so many goods. . . .” It wouldn’t make any sense to you. Whereas if you’d internalized the conventional meaning, you’d be able to follow it perfectly. You’d have no trouble seeing the author’s meaning and why it was funny.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> Which shows that most people do acknowledge a distinction. Otherwise we wouldn’t recognize the first sense as jarring and reject it automatically, whereas we accept the second—the one referring to wealth and the benefits of celebrity and high living—as amusing and apropos.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> Now go a step further. Ask yourself whether we should accept as goods—and should value—the things we have to think of to have the line make sense—the ones whose abundance leaves their owner with “. . . no place to shit.”</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 13. I am made up of substance and what animates it, and neither one can ever stop existing, any more than it began to. Every portion of me will be reassigned as another portion of the world, and that in turn transformed into another. Ad infinitum.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> I was produced through one such transformation, and my parents too, and so on back. Ad infinitum.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> N.B.: Still holds good, even if the world goes through recurrent cycles.</span>

</div>

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 14. The *logos* and its employment are forces sufficient for themselves and for their works. They start from their own beginning, they proceed to the appointed end. We call such activities “directed,” from the directness of their course.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 15. Nothing pertains to human beings except what defines us as human. No other things can be demanded of us. They aren’t proper to human nature, nor is it incomplete without them. It follows that they are not our goal, or what helps us reach it—the good. If any of them were proper to us, it would be improper to disdain or resist it. Nor would we admire people who show themselves immune to it. If the things themselves were good, it could hardly be good to give them up. But in reality the more we deny ourselves such things (and things like them)—or are deprived of them involuntarily, even—the better we become.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 16. The things you think about determine the quality of your mind. Your soul takes on the color of your thoughts. Color it with a run of thoughts like these:</span>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4">\
</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> i. Anywhere you can lead your life, you can lead a good one.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> —Lives are led at court. . . .</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> Then good ones can be.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> ii. Things gravitate toward what they were intended for.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4">\
</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> What things gravitate toward is their goal.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> A thing’s goal is what benefits it—its good.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> A rational being’s good is unselfishness. What we were born for. That’s nothing new. Remember? Lower things for the sake of higher ones, and higher ones for one another. Things that have consciousness are higher than those that don’t. And those with the *logos* still higher.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 17. It is crazy to want what is impossible. And impossible for the wicked not to do so.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 18. Nothing happens to anyone that he can’t endure. The same thing happens to other people, and they weather it unharmed—out of sheer obliviousness or because they want to display “character.” Is wisdom really so much weaker than ignorance and vanity?</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 19. Things have no hold on the soul. They have no access to it, cannot move or direct it. It is moved and directed by itself alone. It takes the things before it and interprets them as it sees fit.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 20. In a sense, people are our proper occupation. Our job is to do them good and put up with them.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> But when they obstruct our proper tasks, they become irrelevant to us—like sun, wind, animals. Our actions may be impeded by them, but there can be no impeding our intentions or our dispositions. Because we can accommodate and adapt. The mind adapts and converts to its own purposes the obstacle to our acting.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> The impediment to action advances action.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> What stands in the way becomes the way.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 21. Honor that which is greatest in the world—that on whose business all things are employed and by whom they are governed.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> And honor what is greatest in yourself: the part that shares its nature with that power. All things—in you as well—are employed about its business, and your life is governed by it.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 22. If it does not harm the community, it does not harm its members.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> When you think you’ve been injured, apply this rule: If the community isn’t injured by it, neither am I. And if it is, anger is not the answer. Show the offender where he went wrong.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 23. Keep in mind how fast things pass by and are gone—those that are now, and those to come. Existence flows past us like a river: the “what” is in constant flux, the “why” has a thousand variations. Nothing is stable, not even what’s right here. The infinity of past and future gapes before us—a chasm whose depths we cannot see.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> So it would take an idiot to feel self-importance or distress. Or any indignation, either. As if the things that irritate us lasted.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 24. Remember:</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> Fate. How small a role you play in it.</span>

<span class="calibre4"> 25. So other people hurt me? That’s their problem. Their character and actions are not mine. What is done to me is ordained by nature, what I do by my own.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 26. The mind is the ruler of the soul. It should remain unstirred by agitations of the flesh—gentle and violent ones alike. Not mingling with them, but fencing itself off and keeping those feelings in their place. When they make their way into your thoughts, through the sympathetic link between mind and body, don’t try to resist the sensation. The sensation is natural. But don’t let the mind start in with judgments, calling it “good” or “bad.”</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 27. “To live with the gods.” And to do that is to show them that your soul accepts what it is given and does what the spirit requires—the spirit God gave each of us to lead and guide us, a fragment of himself. Which is our mind, our *logos*.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 28. Don’t be irritated at people’s smell or bad breath. What’s the point? With that mouth, with those armpits, they’re going to produce that odor.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 29. You can live here as you expect to live there.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> And if they won’t let you, you can depart life now and forfeit nothing. If the smoke makes me cough, I can leave. What’s so hard about that?</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> Until things reach that point, I’m free. No one can keep me from doing what I want. And I want what is proper to rational beings, living together.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 30. The world’s intelligence is not selfish.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> It created lower things for the sake of higher ones, and attuned the higher ones to one another. Look how it subordinates, how it connects, how it assigns each thing what each deserves, and brings the better things into alignment.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 31. How have you behaved to the gods, to your parents, to your siblings, to your wife, to your children, to your teachers, to your nurses, to your friends, to your relatives, to your slaves? Have they all had from you nothing “wrong and unworthy, either word or deed”?</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> Consider all that you’ve gone through, all that you’ve survived. And that the story of your life is done, your assignment complete. How many good things have you seen? How much pain and pleasure have you resisted? How many honors have you declined? How many unkind people have you been kind to?</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 32. Why do other souls—unskilled, untrained—disturb the soul with skill and understanding?</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> —And which is that?</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> 14. Things ordinary people are impressed by fall into the categories of things that are held together by simple physics (like stones or wood), or by natural growth (figs, vines, olives . . .). Those admired by more advanced minds are held together by a living soul (flocks of sheep, herds of cows). Still more sophisticated people admire what is guided by a rational mind—not the universal mind, but one admired for its technical knowledge, or for some other skill—or just because it happens to own a lot of slaves.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 16. What is it in ourselves that we should prize?</span>

</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_10">

<span class="calibre4">\
</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> Then what is to be prized?</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> An audience clapping? No. No more than the clacking of their tongues. Which is all that public praise amounts to—a clacking of tongues.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> So we throw out other people’s recognition. What’s left for us to prize?</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> I think it’s this: to do (and not do) what we were designed for. That’s the goal of all trades, all arts, and what each of them aims at: that the thing they create should do what it was designed to do. The nurseryman who cares for the vines, the horse trainer, the dog breeder—this is what they aim at. And teaching and education—what else are they trying to accomplish?</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> So that’s what we should prize. Hold on to that, and you won’t be tempted to aim at anything else.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> And if you can’t stop prizing a lot of other things? Then you’ll never be free—free, independent, imperturbable. Because you’ll always be envious and jealous, afraid that people might come and take it all away from you. Plotting against those who have them—those things you prize. People who need those things are bound to be a mess—and bound to take out their frustrations on the gods. Whereas to respect your own mind—to prize it—will leave you satisfied with your own self, well integrated into your community and in tune with the gods as well—embracing what they allot you, and what they ordain.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> Take Antoninus as your model, always. His energy in doing what was rational . . . his steadiness in any situation . . . his sense of reverence . . . his calm expression . . . his gentleness . . . his modesty . . . his eagerness to grasp things. And how he never let things go before he was sure he had examined them thoroughly, understood them perfectly . . . the way he put up with unfair criticism, without returning it . . . how he couldn’t be hurried . . . how he wouldn’t listen to informers . . . how reliable he was as a judge of character, and of actions . . . not prone to backbiting, or cowardice, or jealousy, or empty rhetoric . . . content with the basics—in living quarters, bedding, clothes, food, servants . . . how hard he worked, how much he put up with . . . his ability to work straight through till dusk—because of his simple diet (he didn’t even need to relieve himself, except at set times) . . . his constancy and reliability as a friend . . . his tolerance of people who openly questioned his views and his delight at seeing his ideas improved on . . . his piety—without a trace of superstition . . .</span>

<div class="calibre_9">

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 38. Keep reminding yourself of the way things are connected, of their relatedness. All things are implicated in one another and in sympathy with each other. <div class="calibre_8">

<div class="calibre_9">

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 40. Implements, tools, equipment. If they do what they were designed for, then they work. Even if the person who designed them is miles away.</span>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> But with naturally occurring things, the force that designed them is present within them and remains there. Which is why we owe it special reverence, with the recognition that if you live and act as it dictates, then everything in you is intelligently ordered. Just as everything in the world is.</span>

</div>

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 41. You take things you don’t control and define them as “good” or “bad.” And so of course when the “bad” things happen, or the “good” ones don’t, you blame the gods and feel hatred for the people responsible—or those you decide to make responsible. Much of our bad behavior stems from trying to apply those criteria. If we limited “good” and “bad” to our own actions, we’d have no call to challenge God, or to treat other people as enemies.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 42. All of us are working on the same project. Some consciously, with understanding; some without knowing it. (I think this is what Heraclitus meant when he said that “those who sleep are also hard at work”—that they too collaborate in what happens.) Some of us work in one way, and some in others. And those who complain and try to obstruct and thwart things—they help as much as anyone. The world needs them as well.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> So make up your mind who you’ll choose to work with. The force that directs all things will make good use of you regardless—will put you on its payroll and set you to work. But make sure it’s not the job Chrysippus speaks of: the bad line in the play, put there for laughs.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 44. If the gods have made decisions about me and the things that happen to me, then they were good decisions. (It’s hard to picture a god who makes bad ones.) </div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> And if they haven’t made decisions about me as an individual, they certainly have about the general welfare. And anything that follows from that is something I have to welcome and embrace.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> And if they make no decisions, about anything—and it’s blasphemous even to think so (because if so, then let’s stop sacrificing, praying, swearing oaths, and doing all the other things we do, believing the whole time that the gods are right here with us)—if they decide nothing about our lives . . . well, *I* can still make decisions. Can still consider what it’s to my benefit to do. And what benefits anyone is to do what his own nature requires. And mine is rational. Rational and civic.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> My city and state are Rome—as Antoninus. But as a human being? The world. So for me, “good” can only mean what’s good for both communities.</span>

<span class="calibre4"> 1. Evil: the same old thing.</span>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> No matter what happens, keep this in mind: It’s the same old thing, from one end of the world to the other. It fills the history books, ancient and modern, and the cities, and the houses too. Nothing new at all.</span>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> Familiar, transient.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 2. You cannot quench understanding unless you put out the insights that compose it. But you can rekindle those at will, like glowing coals. I can control my thoughts as necessary; then how can I be troubled? What is outside my mind means nothing to it. Absorb that lesson and your feet stand firm.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> You can return to life. Look at things as you did before. And life returns.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

</div>

<span class="calibre4"> 33. \[On pain:\] Unendurable pain brings its own end with it. Chronic pain is always endurable: the intelligence maintains serenity by cutting itself off from the body, the mind remains undiminished. And the parts that pain affects—let them speak for themselves, if they can.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 34. \[On Ambition:\] How their minds work, the things they long for and fear. Events like piles of sand, drift upon drift—each one soon hidden by the next.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 35. “ ‘If his mind is filled with nobility, with a grasp of all time, all existence, do you think our human life will mean much to him at all?’</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> “ ‘How could it?’ he said.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> “ ‘Or death be very frightening?’</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> “ ‘Not in the least.’ ”</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 36. “Kingship: to earn a bad reputation by good deeds.”</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 37. Disgraceful: that the mind should control the face, should be able to shape and mold it as it pleases, but not shape and mold itself.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 38. “And why should we feel anger at the world? As if the world would notice!”</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 39. “May you bring joy to us and those on high.”</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 40. “To harvest life like standing stalks of grain Grown and cut down in turn.”</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 41. “If I and my two children cannot move the gods The gods must have their reasons.”</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 42. “For what is just and good is on my side.”</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 43. No chorus of lamentation, no hysterics.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 44. “Then the only proper response for me to make is this: ‘You are much mistaken, my friend, if you think that any man worth his salt cares about the risk of death and doesn’t concentrate on this alone: whether what he’s doing is right or wrong, and his behavior a good man’s or a bad one’s.’ ”</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 45. “It’s like this, gentlemen of the jury: The spot where a person decides to station himself, or wherever his commanding officer stations him—well, I think that’s where he ought to take his stand and face the enemy, and not worry about being killed, or about anything but doing his duty.”</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 46. “But, my good friend, consider the possibility that nobility and virtue are not synonymous with the loss or preservation of one’s life. Is it not possible that a real man should forget about living a certain number of years, and should not cling to life, but leave it up to the gods, accepting, as women say, that ‘no one can escape his fate,’ and turn his attention to how he can best live the life before him?”</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 47. To watch the courses of the stars as if you revolved with them. To keep constantly in mind how the elements alter into one another. Thoughts like this wash off the mud of life below.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 48. \[Plato has it right.\] If you want to talk about people, you need to look down on the earth from above. </div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 49. Look at the past—empire succeeding empire—and from that, extrapolate the future: the same thing. No escape from the rhythm of events.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 52. A better wrestler. But not a better citizen, a better person, a better resource in tight places, a better forgiver of faults.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 53. Wherever something can be done as the *logos* shared by gods and men dictates, there all is in order. Where there is profit because our effort is productive, because it advances in step with our nature, there we have nothing to fear.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 54. Everywhere, at each moment, you have the option:</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_13">

<span class="calibre4">\
</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_13">

<span class="calibre4"> • to accept this event with humility</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_13">

<span class="calibre4"> • to treat this person as he should be treated</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_13">

<span class="calibre4"> • to approach this thought with care, so that nothing irrational creeps in.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_13">

<span class="calibre4">\
</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 55. Don’t pay attention to other people’s minds. Look straight ahead, where nature is leading you—nature in general, through the things that happen to you; and your own nature, through your own actions.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> Everything has to do what it was made for. And other things were made for those with *logos.* In this respect as in others: lower things exist for the sake of higher ones, and higher things for one another.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> Now, the main thing we were made for is to work with others.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> Secondly, to resist our body’s urges. Because things driven by *logos*—by thought—have the capacity for detachment—to resist impulses and sensations, both of which are merely corporeal. Thought seeks to be their master, not their subject. And so it should: they were created for its use.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> And the third thing is to avoid rashness and credulity.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> The mind that grasps this and steers straight ahead should be able to hold its own.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 56. Think of yourself as dead. You have lived your life. Now take what’s left and live it properly.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 57. To love only what happens, what was destined. No greater harmony.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 58. In all that happens, keep before your eyes those who experienced it before you, and felt shock and outrage and resentment at it.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> And now where are they? Nowhere.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> Is that what you want to be like? Instead of avoiding all these distracting assaults—leaving the alarms and flight to others—and concentrating on what you can *do* with it all?</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> Because you can use it, treat it as raw material. Just pay attention, and resolve to live up to your own expectations. In everything. And when faced with a choice, remember: our business is with things that really matter.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 59. Dig deep; the water—goodness—is down there. And as long as you keep digging, it will keep bubbling up.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 60. What the body needs is stability. To be impervious to jolts in all it is and does. The cohesiveness and beauty that intelligence lends to the face—that’s what the body needs.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> But it should come without effort.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 61. Not a dancer but a wrestler: waiting, poised and dug in, for sudden assaults.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 62. Look at who they really are, the people whose approval you long for, and what their minds are really like. Then you won’t blame the ones who make mistakes they can’t help, and you won’t feel a need for their approval. You will have seen the sources of both—their judgments and their actions.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 63. “Against our will, our souls are cut off from truth.”</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> Truth, yes, and justice, self-control, kindness . . .</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> Important to keep this in mind. It will make you more patient with other people.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 64. For times when you feel pain:</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> See that it doesn’t disgrace you, or degrade your intelligence—doesn’t keep it from acting rationally or unselfishly.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> And in most cases what Epicurus said should help: that pain is neither unbearable nor unending, as long as you keep in mind its limits and don’t magnify them in your imagination.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> And keep in mind too that pain often comes in disguise—as drowsiness, fever, loss of appetite. . . . When you’re bothered by things like that, remind yourself: “I’m giving in to pain.”</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 65. Take care that you don’t treat inhumanity as it treats human beings.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 66. How do we know that Telauges wasn’t a better man than Socrates?</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> It’s not enough to ask whether Socrates’ death was nobler, whether he debated with the sophists more adeptly, whether he showed greater endurance by spending the night out in the cold, and when he was ordered to arrest the man from Salamis decided it was preferable to refuse, and “swaggered about the streets” (which one could reasonably doubt).</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> What matters is what kind of soul he had.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> Whether he was satisfied to treat men with justice and the gods with reverence and didn’t lose his temper unpredictably at evil done by others, didn’t make himself the slave of other people’s ignorance, didn’t treat anything that nature did as abnormal, or put up with it as an unbearable imposition, didn’t put his mind in his body’s keeping.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 67. Nature did not blend things so inextricably that you can’t draw your own boundaries—place your own well-being in your own hands. It’s quite possible to be a good man without anyone realizing it. Remember that.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> And this too: you don’t need much to live happily. And just because you’ve abandoned your hopes of becoming a great thinker or scientist, don’t give up on attaining freedom, achieving humility, serving others, obeying God.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 68. To live life in peace, immune to all compulsion. Let them scream whatever they want. Let animals dismember this soft flesh that covers you. How would any of that stop you from keeping your mind calm—reliably sizing up what’s around you—and ready to make good use of whatever happens? So that Judgment can look the event in the eye and say, “This is what you really are, regardless of what you may look like.” While Adaptability adds, “You’re just what I was looking for.” Because to me the present is a chance for the exercise of rational virtue—civic virtue—in short, the art that men share with gods. Both treat whatever happens as wholly natural; not novel or hard to deal with, but familiar and easily handled.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 69. Perfection of character: to live your last day, every day, without frenzy, or sloth, or pretense.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 70. The gods live forever and yet they don’t seem annoyed at having to put up with human beings and their behavior throughout eternity. And not only put up with but actively care for them.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> And you—on the verge of death—you still refuse to care for them, although you’re one of them yourself.</span>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 71. It’s silly to try to escape other people’s faults. They are inescapable. Just try to escape your own.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 72. Whenever the force that makes us rational and social encounters something that is neither, then it can reasonably regard it as inferior.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 73. You’ve given aid and they’ve received it. And yet, like an idiot, you keep holding out for more: to be credited with a Good Deed, to be repaid in kind. Why?</span>

</div>

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 74. No one objects to what is useful to him.</span>

<div class="calibre_9">

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 1. Another encouragement to humility: you can’t claim to have lived your life as a philosopher—not even your whole adulthood. You can see for yourself how far you are from philosophy. And so can many others. You’re tainted. It’s not so easy now—to have a reputation as a philosopher. And your position is an obstacle as well.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> So you know how things stand. Now forget what they think of you. Be satisfied if you can live the rest of your life, however short, as your nature demands. Focus on that, and don’t let anything distract you. You’ve wandered all over and finally realized that you never found what you were after: how to live. Not in syllogisms, not in money, or fame, or self-indulgence. Nowhere.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> —Then where is it to be found?</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> In doing what human nature requires.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> —How?</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> Through first principles. Which should govern your intentions and your actions.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> —What principles?</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> Those to do with good and evil. That nothing is good except what leads to fairness, and self-control, and courage, and free will. And nothing bad except what does the opposite.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 2. For every action, ask: How does it affect me? Could I change my mind about it?</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> But soon I’ll be dead, and the slate’s empty. So this is the only question: Is it the action of a responsible being, part of society, and subject to the same decrees as God?</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 3. Alexander and Caesar and Pompey. Compared with Diogenes, Heraclitus, Socrates? The philosophers knew the what, the why, the how. Their minds were their own.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> The others? Nothing but anxiety and enslavement.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 4. You can hold your breath until you turn blue, but they’ll still go on doing it.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 5. The first step: Don’t be anxious. Nature controls it all. And before long you’ll be no one, nowhere—like Hadrian, like Augustus.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> The second step: Concentrate on what you have to do. Fix your eyes on it. Remind yourself that your task is to be a good human being; remind yourself what nature demands of people. Then do it, without hesitation, and speak the truth as you see it. But with kindness. With humility. Without hypocrisy.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 6. Nature’s job: to shift things elsewhere, to transform them, to pick them up and move them here and there. Constant alteration. But not to worry: there’s nothing new here. Everything is familiar. Even the proportions are unchanged.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 7. Nature of any kind thrives on forward progress. And progress for a rational mind means not accepting falsehood or uncertainty in its perceptions, making unselfish actions its only aim, seeking and shunning only the things it has control over, embracing what nature demands of it—the nature in which it participates, as the leaf’s nature does in the tree’s. Except that the nature shared by the leaf is without consciousness or reason, and subject to impediments. Whereas that shared by human beings is without impediments, and rational, and just, since it allots to each and every thing an equal and proportionate share of time, being, purpose, action, chance. Examine it closely. Not whether they’re identical point by point, but in the aggregate: this weighed against that.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 8. No time for reading. For controlling your arrogance, yes. For overcoming pain and pleasure, yes. For outgrowing ambition, yes. For not feeling anger at stupid and unpleasant people—even for caring about them—for that, yes.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 9. Don’t be overheard complaining about life at court. Not even to yourself.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 10. Remorse is annoyance at yourself for having passed up something that’s to your benefit. But if it’s to your benefit it must be good—something a truly good person would be concerned about.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> But no truly good person would feel remorse at passing up pleasure.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> So it cannot be to your benefit, or good.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 11. What is this, fundamentally? What is its nature and substance, its reason for being? What is it doing in the world? How long is it here for?</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 12. When you have trouble getting out of bed in the morning, remember that your defining characteristic—what defines a human being—is to work with others. Even animals know how to sleep. And it’s the characteristic activity that’s the more natural one—more innate and more satisfying.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 13. Apply them constantly, to everything that happens: Physics. Ethics. Logic.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 14. When you have to deal with someone, ask yourself: What does he mean by good and bad? </div>

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 57. We speak of the sun’s light as “pouring down on us,” as “pouring over us” in all directions. Yet it’s never poured out. Because it doesn’t really pour; it extends. Its beams (*aktai*) get their name from their extension (*ekteinesthai*).</span>

<div class="calibre_9">

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> 1. Injustice is a kind of blasphemy. Nature designed rational beings for each other’s sake: to help—not harm—one another, as they deserve. To transgress its will, then, is to blaspheme against the oldest of the gods.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> And to lie is to blaspheme against it too. Because “nature” means the nature of that which is. And that which is and that which is the case are closely linked, so that nature is synonymous with Truth—the source of all true things. To lie deliberately is to blaspheme—the liar commits deceit, and thus injustice. And likewise to lie without realizing it. Because the involuntary liar disrupts the harmony of nature—its order. He is in conflict with the way the world is structured. As anyone is who deviates toward what is opposed to the truth—even against his will. Nature gave him the resources to distinguish between true and false. And he neglected them, and now can’t tell the difference.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> And to pursue pleasure as good, and flee from pain as evil—that too is blasphemous. Someone who does that is bound to find himself constantly reproaching nature—complaining that it doesn’t treat the good and bad as they deserve, but often lets the bad enjoy pleasure and the things that produce it, and makes the good suffer pain, and the things that produce pain. And moreover, to fear pain is to fear something that’s bound to happen, the world being what it is—and that again is blasphemy. While if you pursue pleasure, you can hardly avoid wrongdoing—which is manifestly blasphemous.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

Because that’s all that could restrain us (if anything could)—the only thing that could make us want to stay here: the chance to live with those who share our vision. But now? <div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> Then don’t ask the impossible. There have to be shameless people in the world. This is one of them.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> The same for someone vicious or untrustworthy, or with any other defect. Remembering that the whole class has to exist will make you more tolerant of its members.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> Another useful point to bear in mind: What qualities has nature given us to counter that defect? As an antidote to unkindness it gave us kindness. And other qualities to balance other flaws.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> And when others stray off course, you can always try to set them straight, because every wrongdoer is doing something wrong—doing something *the wrong way.*</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> And how does it injure you anyway? You’ll find that none of the people you’re upset about has done anything that could do damage to your mind. But that’s all that “harm” or “injury” could mean. Yes, boorish people do boorish things. What’s strange or unheard-of about that? Isn’t it yourself you should reproach—for not anticipating that they’d act this way? The *logos* gave you the means to see it—that a given person would act a given way—but you paid no attention. And now you’re astonished that he’s gone and done it. So when you call someone “untrustworthy” or “ungrateful,” turn the reproach on yourself. It was *you* who did wrong. By assuming that someone with those traits deserved your trust. Or by doing them a favor and expecting something in return, instead of looking to the action itself for your reward. What else did you expect from helping someone out? Isn’t it enough that you’ve done what your nature demands? You want a salary for it too? As if your eyes expected a reward for seeing, or your feet for walking. That’s what they were made for. By doing what they were designed to do, they’re performing their function. Whereas humans were made to help others. And when we do help others—or help them to do something—we’re doing what we were designed for. We perform our function.</span>

</div>
