<span class="calibre4"> And further . . . </span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_10">

<span class="calibre4">\
</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_10">

<span class="calibre4"> That whatever happens has always happened, and always will, and is happening at this very moment, everywhere. Just like this.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_10">

<span class="calibre4"> What links one human being to all humans: not blood, or birth, but mind.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_10">

<span class="calibre4">\
</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> And . . . </span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_10">

<span class="calibre4">\
</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_10">

<span class="calibre4"> That an individual’s mind is God and of God.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_10">

<span class="calibre4"> That nothing belongs to anyone. Children, body, life itself—all of them come from that same source.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_10">

<span class="calibre4"> That it’s all how you choose to see things.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_10">

<span class="calibre4"> That the present is all we have to live in. Or to lose.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 27. Constantly run down the list of those who felt intense anger at something: the most famous, the most unfortunate, the most hated, the most whatever. And ask: Where is all that now? Smoke, dust, legend . . . or not even a legend. Think of all the examples: Fabius Catullinus in the country, Lusius Lupus in the orchard, Stertinius at Baiae, Tiberius on Capri, Velius Rufus . . . obsession and arrogance.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> And how trivial the things we want so passionately are. And how much more philosophical it would be to take what we’re given and show uprightness, self-control, obedience to God, without making a production of it. There’s nothing more insufferable than people who boast about their own humility.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 28. People ask, “Have you ever seen the gods you worship? How can you be sure they exist?”</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> Answers:</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4">\
</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> i. Just look around you.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> ii. I’ve never seen my soul either. And yet I revere it.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4">\
</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> That’s how I know the gods exist and why I revere them—from having felt their power, over and over.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 29. Salvation: to see each thing for what it is—its nature and its purpose.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> To do only what is right, say only what is true, without holding back.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> What else could it be but to live life fully—to pay out goodness like the rings of a chain, without the slightest gap.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 30. Singular, not plural:</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> Sunlight. Though broken up by walls and mountains and a thousand other things.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> Substance. Though split into a thousand forms, variously shaped.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> Life. Though distributed among a thousand different natures with their individual limitations.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> Intelligence. Even if it seems to be divided.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> The other components—breath, matter—lack any awareness or connection to one another (yet unity and its gravitational pull embrace them too).</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> But intelligence is uniquely drawn toward what is akin to it, and joins with it inseparably, in shared awareness.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 31. What is it you want? To keep on breathing? What about feeling? desiring? growing? ceasing to grow? using your voice? thinking? Which of them seems worth having?</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> But if you can do without them all, then continue to follow the *logos,* and God. To the end. To prize those other things—to grieve because death deprives us of them—is an obstacle.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 32. The fraction of infinity, of that vast abyss of time, allotted to each of us. Absorbed in an instant into eternity.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> The fraction of all substance, and all spirit.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> The fraction of the whole earth you crawl about on.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> Keep all that in mind, and don’t treat anything as important except doing what your nature demands, and accepting what Nature sends you.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 33. How the mind conducts itself. It all depends on that. All the rest is within its power, or beyond its control—corpses and smoke.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 34. An incentive to treat death as unimportant: even people whose only morality is pain and pleasure can manage that much.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 35. If you make ripeness alone your good . . .</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> If a few actions more or less, governed by the right *logos,* are merely a few more or less . . .</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> If it makes no difference whether you look at the world for this long or that long . . .</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> . . . then death shouldn’t scare you.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> 36. You’ve lived as a citizen in a great city. Five years or a hundred—what’s the difference? The laws make no distinction.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> And to be sent away from it, not by a tyrant or a dishonest judge, but by Nature, who first invited you in—why is that so terrible?</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> Like the impresario ringing down the curtain on an actor:</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> “But I’ve only gotten through three acts . . . !”</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> Yes. This will be a drama in three acts, the length fixed by the power that directed your creation, and now directs your dissolution. Neither was yours to determine.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_9">

<span class="calibre4"> So make your exit with grace—the same grace shown to you.</span>

</div>

<div id="index_split_029.html_calibre_pb_41" class="mbp_pagebreak">

</div>

<span id="index_split_030.html"></span>

<div id="index_split_030.html_filepos460745" class="calibre_4">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_2">

<span class="calibre2"> Notes</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_3">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 1.1 *My grandfather Verus:* Verus (1).</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 1.2 *My father:* Verus (2).</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 1.3 *My mother:* Lucilla.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 1.4 *My great-grandfather:* Severus (1).</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre6"> *To avoid the public schools:* Roman aristocrats normally preferred to have their sons educated by private tutors (often specially trained household slaves) who were considered safer and more reliable than the professional schoolmasters who taught all comers for a fee.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 1.5 *My first teacher:* Not named and most likely a slave.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre6"> *Not to support this side or that:* Literally, “not to be a Green or a Blue; not to support the *parmularius* \[a gladiator with a small shield\] or the *scutarius* \[who carried a larger shield\].”</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 1.6 *the camp-bed and the cloak:* Symbols of an ascetic lifestyle. Marcus’s sleeping arrangements are recorded by the *Historia Augusta:* “He used to sleep on the ground, and his mother had a hard time convincing him to sleep on a cot spread with skins.”</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 1.7 *his own copy:* It is unclear whether this refers to Arrian’s *Discourses of Epictetus* or to a set of unpublished notes, perhaps taken by Rusticus himself.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 1.13 *Domitius and Athenodotus:* The anecdote Marcus refers to is unknown.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 1.14 *My brother:* Probably a copyist’s error based on confusion between the names Verus and Severus.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre6"> *Thrasea, Helvidius, Cato:* For the significance of these three figures as Stoic exemplars see the Introduction.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 1.16 *My adopted father:* Antoninus Pius. The sketch here seems to be a development and expansion of the briefer assessment in 6.30.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre6"> *Putting a stop to the pursuit of boys:* This may be meant as a critique of Antoninus’s predecessor, Hadrian (2), whose love affair with the youth Antinoüs was notorious. Alternatively it might refer to legal restrictions on pederasty (which was common in upper-class Greek and Roman society), or to Antoninus’s own self-restraint.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre6"> *The robe* *.* *.* *.* *the customs agent’s apology:* These examples of Antoninus’s modesty are too compressed and allusive to be intelligible to anyone but Marcus himself.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre6"> *as they say of Socrates:* Marcus may be recalling a similar comment by Xenophon, *Memorabilia* 1.3.14; Socrates’ ability to drink heavily without any apparent effect is celebrated in Plato’s *Symposium* (179c, 220a).</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre6"> *Maximus’s illness:* For Maximus see the Index of Persons; nothing is known of his illness.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 1.17 *someone:* Antoninus.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre6"> *the kind of brother:* Verus (3).</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre6"> *the honors they seemed to want:* Marcus may be thinking of Herodes Atticus and Fronto, both of whom held consulships in 143, soon after Marcus became the heir apparent. Perhaps also of Rusticus, who held a second consulship in 162.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre6"> *I never laid a finger:* Household slaves were often exposed to sexual abuse at the hands of their owners.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre6"> *That I have the wife I do:* Faustina.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre6"> *at Caieta:* A seaport on the west coast of Italy. The Greek text adds an unintelligible phrase, which some scholars interpret as a reference to “an oracle.”</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre6"> *“we need the help* *.* *.* *.”:* Apparently a quotation, but not from any surviving work.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 2. *On the River Gran, Among the Quadi:* The notation is transmitted at the end of Book 1, but is more likely to belong here. The Gran (or Hron) is a tributary of the Danube flowing through modern-day Slovakia. The Quadi were a Suebian tribe in the Morava River valley, subdued during the Marcomannic Wars of the early 170s.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 2.2 *Throw away* *.* *.* *.* *right now:* These words are deleted or transposed elsewhere by some editors.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 2.10 *the ones committed out of desire are worse:* Strictly speaking, this assessment is in conflict with Stoic doctrine, which holds that there are no degrees of wrongness; all wrong actions are equally wrong and it makes no sense to speak of one as being “worse” than another.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 2.13 *“delving into* *.* *.* *.”:* A line from the lyric poet Pindar (frg. 282), quoted also by Plato, *Theaetetus* 173e.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 3. *In Carnuntum:* Transmitted at the end of Book 2, but probably meant to head Book 3. Carnuntum was a fortress on the Danube which housed the Legio XIV Gemina and served as the seat of the governor of Upper Pannonia. Marcus is known to have been in the area in 172 and 173.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 3.3 *Chaldaeans:* The Chaldaeans (Babylonians) had a special reputation as astrologers.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre6"> *Democritus:* Apparently an error for another pre-Socratic philosopher, Pherecydes, who was said to have been eaten by worms. (Democritus’s name was often coupled with that of Heraclitus, which may explain Marcus’s slip here.)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre6"> *Socrates:* The “vermin” who killed Socrates are the Athenians who prosecuted and condemned him.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 3.6 *as Socrates used to say:* It is not clear whether Marcus is alluding to a specific passage (perhaps Plato, *Phaedo* 83a–b) or merely to a general impression of Socratic doctrine.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 3.14 *your* Brief Comments*:* Evidently collections of anecdotes and/or quotations put together by Marcus himself for his own use, like parts of the extant *Meditations.*</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 3.15 *They don’t realize* *.* *.* *.* *:* The significance of this entry (particularly the last phrase) is unclear.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 3.16 *people who do* \< *.* *.* *.* \>*:* It seems clear that something is missing from the text, perhaps deliberately omitted by a prudish copyist.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 4.3 *to ward off all* \< *.* *.* *.* \>*:* The missing word must be something like “anxiety.”</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre6"> *“The world is nothing but change* *.* *.* *.”:* Democritus frg. B 115.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 4.18 \< *.* *.* *.* \> *not to be distracted:* The text as transmitted includes the words “good,” “black character,” and “suspicion,” but no coherent sense can be made of them.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 4.19 *You’re out of step* *.* *.* *.* *:* The text of this sentence is disturbed and the translation correspondingly uncertain.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 4.23 *The poet:* Aristophanes frg. 112.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 4.24 *“If you seek tranquillity* *.* *.* *.”:* Democritus frg. B 3.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 4.30 *A philosopher without clothes* *.* *.* *.* *:* If the text is sound it is not easy to interpret convincingly. The rendering here (which differs from most previous versions) represents my best guess at the sense, but is far from certain.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 4.33 *Camillus, Caeso, Volesus, Dentatus:* Heroes of the Roman Republic (see the Index of Persons). Only Camillus was well known; the others may have been purposely chosen for their obscurity.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre6"> *“unknown, unasked-for”:* Homer, *Odyssey* 1.242.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 4.41 *“A little wisp of soul* *.* *.* *.”:* Epictetus frg. 26 (presumably from one of the lost books of the *Discourses*).</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 4.46 *“When earth dies* *.* *.* *.”:* Heraclitus frg. B 76.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre6"> *“Those who have forgotten* *.* *.* *.”:* idem. frg. B 71.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre6"> *“They are at odds* *.* *.* *.”:* idem. frg. B 72.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre6"> *“they find alien* *.* *.* *.”:* idem. frg. B 73.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre6"> *“Our words and actions* *.* *.* *.”:* idem. frg. B 74.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 4.48 *Helike, Pompeii, Herculaneum:* Helike was a Greek city destroyed by an earthquake and tidal wave in 373 <span class="small">B.C.</span> Pompeii and its neighbor city Herculaneum were destroyed by the eruption of Mount Vesuvius in <span class="small">A.D.</span> 79.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 4.49a *It’s unfortunate:* It has been plausibly suggested that this entry is a quotation from a lost section of Epictetus’s *Discourses.*</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 4.50 *Caedicianus, Fabius, Julian, Lepidus:* With the possible exception of Caedicianus and Lepidus (see the Index of Persons), none of these figures can be identified.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 5.8 *“the doctor”:* Literally, “Asclepius.” Patients sleeping in his temple sometimes had dream visions of the god and received suggestions for treatment from him. But the name might simply indicate a human physician.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 5.10 *a pervert:* The Greek word (used also in 6.34) is a contemptuous term referring to the passive partner in homosexual intercourse; it has no exact English equivalent (“pervert,” although overly broad, at least has the right tone). Marcus is probably using it as a generalized term of abuse.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 5.12 *“so many goods* *.* *.* *.”:* Proverbial: the rich man owns “so many goods he has no place to shit.” The saying is at least as old as the fourth-century <span class="small">B.C.</span> comic poet Menander, who quotes it in the surviving fragments of his play *The Apparition.*</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 5.29 *If the smoke makes me cough:* The metaphor is drawn from Epictetus, *Discourses* 1.25.18.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 5.31 *“wrong and unworthy* *.* *.* *.”:* Homer, *Odyssey* 4.690.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 5.33 *“gone from the earth* *.* *.* *.”:* Hesiod, *Works and Days* 197.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 5.36 *Not to be overwhelmed:* The remainder of this book is unintelligible in places, perhaps because the end of the original papyrus roll suffered accidental damage. I have divided the text into three separate sections, but without great confidence that this is correct.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre6"> *Like the old man:* The reference is obscure. A scene from a lost tragedy?</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 6.13 *Crates on Xenocrates:* The meaning of this reference is unknown.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 6.30 *Take Antoninus as your model:* The sketch that follows seems to be a preliminary version of the longer portrait at 1.16.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 6.34 *perverts:* See 5.10 note.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 6.42 “*those who sleep* *.* *.* *.”:* Heraclitus frg. B 75.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre6"> *the bad line in the play:* Chrysippus frg. 1181 (= Plutarch, *On Stoic Self-Contradictions* 13f.). Chrysippus compared the existence of evil to a deliberately bathetic line in a comedy—bad in itself, but an essential part of a good play.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 7.12 *not:* The transmitted text reads “or,” but this can hardly be correct (compare 3.5).</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 7.15 *Like gold or emerald or purple:* Compare Epictetus, *Discourses* 1.2.17–18: “You see yourself as one thread in a garment . . . But I want to be the purple thread, the small, glistening one that enhances the others.”</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 7.24 “ *.* *.* *.* “ *or in the end is put out:* I have omitted a short phrase from which it is impossible to extract any meaning.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 7.31a *“.* *.* *.* *all are relative* *.* *.* *.”:* A paraphrase of Democritus frg. B 9, in which qualities like sweetness or bitterness are said to be “relative” or “conventional” rather than inherent (what tastes sweet to one person may be bitter to another). Marcus apparently sees the observation as compatible with the Stoic doctrine that “it’s all in how you perceive it” (12.8), though he naturally rejects the subsequent reference to atoms. The final phrase is corrupt beyond repair.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 7.32 *\[On death\]:* The headings of this and the next two entries are probably not Marcus’s own, but additions by a later reader.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 7.35 *“If his mind is filled* *.* *.* *.”:* Plato, *Republic* 6.486a.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 7.36 “*Kingship* *.* *.* *.”:* Antisthenes frg. 20b (also quoted by Epictetus, *Discourses* 4.6.20).</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 7.38 *“And why should we feel anger* *.* *.* *.”:* Euripides, frg. 287 (from the lost *Bellerophon* quoted also at 11.6).</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 7.39 *“May you bring joy* *.* *.* *.”:* Source unknown; perhaps from a lost epic.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 7.40 *“To harvest life* *.* *.* *.”:* Euripides, frg. 757 (from the lost *Hypsipyle*).</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 7.41 *“If I and my two children* *.* *.* *.”:* Euripides, frg. 208 (from the lost *Antiope;* quoted also at 11.6).</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 7.42 *“For what is just and good* *.* *.* *.”:* Ibid., frg. 918 (from an unknown play).</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 7.43 *No chorus of lamentation:* This might be a quotation, like the preceding entries, but if so, we do not know its source.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 7.44 *“Then the only proper response* *.* *.* *.”:* Plato, *Apology* 28b.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 7.45 *“It’s like this* *.* *.* *.”:* Ibid., 28d.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 7.46 *“But, my good friend* *.* *.* *.”:* Plato, *Gorgias* 512d.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 7.48 *\[Plato has it right\]:* The passage that follows does not correspond to anything in Plato’s preserved writings, and it seems likely that the phrase was inserted by a later reader who mistook it for a quotation.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 7.50 *“Earth’s offspring* *.* *.* *.”:* Euripides, frg. 839 (from the lost *Chrysippus*).</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 7.51 *“.* *.* *.* *with food and drink* *.* *.* *.”:* Euripides, *Suppliants* 1110–1111.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 7.51a *“To labor cheerfully* *.* *.* *.”:* From an unknown tragedy.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 7.63 *“Against our will* *.* *.* *.”:* Epictetus, *Discourses* 1.28.4 (also 2.22.37), paraphrasing Plato, *Sophist* 228c.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 7.64 *what Epicurus said:* Epicurus frg. 447.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 7.66 *by spending the night out in the cold:* This anecdote is told by Alcibiades in Plato’s *Symposium* (220).</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre6"> *the man from Salamis:* During the brief reign of the “Thirty Tyrants” at Athens, Socrates was ordered to collaborate with the regime by arresting a certain Leon, but refused; the story is told in Plato’s *Apology* (32c).</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre6"> *“swaggered about the streets”:* A line from Aristophanes’ comedy *Clouds* (362), which pokes fun at Socrates.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 8.25 *Verus* *.* *.* *.* *Lucilla:* Marcus’s parents.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre6"> *Hadrian:* Most likely this refers to the rhetorician (Hadrian 1) rather than the emperor (Hadrian 2).</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 8.35 *We have various abilities* *.* *.* *.* *:* The text here appears to be corrupt and the translation is necessarily uncertain.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 8.38 *Look at it clearly:* The text, meaning and articulation of entries 38 and 39 are very uncertain. Earlier editors printed the opening of 38 as the end of 37, and took the phrases “Look at it clearly—if you can” and “To the best of my judgment” as a single unit, though the resulting sentence yields no coherent sense. I follow J. Dalfen in separating them.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 8.39 *“To the best of my judgment* *.* *.* *.”:* I have placed the entry in quotation marks on the basis of the opening phrase, which includes a parenthetical “he \[or “someone”\] says.” This assumes that the phrase is correctly transmitted (it is certainly not easy to construe), and that it should be taken with what follows rather than what precedes, which is far from certain (see previous note). However, the entry as a whole (an implicit criticism of the Epicureans’ view of pleasure as the supreme good) does not strike me as being typical of Marcus’s style, and I suspect he may indeed be quoting some earlier writer.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 8.41 *“a sphere* *.* *.* *.”:* Empedocles frg. B27, quoted in fuller form at 12.3.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 8.57 *Its beams get their name* *.* *.* *.* *:* This (false) derivation is a typical example of ancient etymology, a science in which the early Stoics were much interested.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 9.2 *the “next best voyage”:* A proverbial phrase meaning having to row when one cannot sail.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 9.24 *“Odysseus in the Underworld”:* The reference is to Book 11 of the *Odyssey,* in which Odysseus descends to Hades and encounters the shades of his companions who died at Troy.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 9.29 *Demetrius of Phalerum:* It has been suggested that “of Phalerum” is a later reader’s (mistaken) addition, and that Marcus had in mind the Hellenistic monarch Demetrius Poliorcetes (“the city-sacker”). But there seems no reason to doubt the transmitted text.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 9.41 *“During my illness* *.* *.* *.”:* Epicurus frg. 191.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 10.10 *Sarmatians:* One of the barbarian tribes Marcus spent his last decade fighting.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 10.21 *“The earth knows longing* *.* *.* *.”:* Euripides frg. 898.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 10.23 *“fencing a sheepfold* *.* *.* *.”:* A paraphrase of Plato, *Theaetetus* 174d, in which we are told that the philosopher will look down on a king as if the latter were a humble shepherd.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 10.31 *When you look* *.* *.* *.* *:* Most of the names mentioned here are mere ciphers (see the Index of Persons for the best guess as to their identities), but Marcus’s point does not depend on knowledge of the individuals.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 10.33 *as a cylinder rolls down:* The comparison is taken from Chrysippus frg. 1000.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 10.34 *“.* *.* *.* *leaves that the wind* *.* *.* *.”:* Homer, *Iliad* 6.147 ff., a very famous passage.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 11.3 *\[like the Christians\]:* This ungrammatical phrase is almost certainly a marginal comment by a later reader; there is no reason to think Marcus had the Christians in mind here. (See Introduction.)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 11.6 *“o Mount Cithaeron!”:* Sophocles, *Oedipus the King* 1391 (Oedipus’s anguished cry after blinding himself, invoking the mountain he was abandoned on as a baby.)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre6"> *“If I and my two children* *.* *.* *.”:* See on 7.41.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre6"> *“And why should we feel anger* *.* *.* *.* *?”:* See on 7.38.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre6"> *“To harvest life* *.* *.* *.”:* See on 7.40.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 11.18 *from Apollo:* Often depicted as the leader of the nine Muses.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 11.22 *The town mouse:* Aesop, *Fables* 297. The significance of the allusion is unclear.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 11.23 *“the monsters under the bed”:* Plato, *Crito* 46c and *Phaedo* 77e; Marcus is probably drawing on Epictetus, *Discourses* 2.1.14.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 11.25 *Perdiccas’s invitation:* In fact the ruler who invited Socrates to his court was Perdiccas’s successor Archelaus (resigned 413–399).</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 11.26 *This advice:* Epicurus frg. 210.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 11.28 *Socrates dressed in a towel:* The anecdote is not preserved.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 11.30 *“For you/Are but a slave* *.* *.* *.”:* From a lost tragedy. Marcus twists what must have been the sense of the original (“it is not for you to speak”) by taking *logos* in its broader, philosophical sense.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 11.31 *“But my heart rejoiced”:* Homer, *Odyssey* 9.413.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 11.32 *“And jeer at virtue* *.* *.* *.”:* Hesiod, *Works and Days* 186, but “virtue” is Marcus’s substitution. Hesiod has “and jeer at them,” in a completely different context.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 11.33 *Stupidity is expecting figs:* A paraphrase of Epictetus, *Discourses* 3.24.86.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 11.34 *As you kiss your son:* Ibid., 3.24.88.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 11.36 *“No thefts of free will* *.* *.* *.”:* Ibid., 3.22.105 (the attribution in the text is probably an addition by a later reader who recognized the quotation).</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 11.37 *“We need to master* *.* *.* *.”:* Ibid., frg. 27.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 11.38 *“This is not a debate* *.* *.* *.”:* Ibid., frg. 28.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 11.39 *Socrates: What do you want?:* Source uncertain: perhaps from a lost section of Epictetus.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 12.3 *“a sphere rejoicing* *.* *.* *.”:* Empedocles frg. B 27 (also quoted at 8.41).</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 12.11a *What it’s made of:* Part of 12 in the manuscripts; placed in 11 by Meric Casaubon. Perhaps an incomplete entry, perhaps an addition by a later hand.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 12.17 *Let your intention be* \< *.* *.* *.* \>*:* The division between Chapters 17 and 18 is unclear, and it seems likely that some text has been lost.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 12.27 *Fabius Catullinus et al.:* Most of the references are obscure; see the Index of Persons for what can be guessed of them.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_14">

<span class="calibre6"> 12.34 *people whose only morality* *.* *.* *.* *:* The Epicureans.</span>

</div>

<div id="index_split_030.html_calibre_pb_42" class="mbp_pagebreak">

</div>

<span id="index_split_031.html"></span>

<div id="index_split_031.html_filepos496206" class="calibre_4">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_2">

<span class="calibre2"> Index of Persons</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_3">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre6"> This list covers only persons named, referred to, or quoted in the text of the *Meditations* itself.</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> A<span class="small">GRIPPA:</span> Roman general; adviser and close associate of A<span class="small">UGUSTUS</span>, whose daughter he married. (8.31)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> A<span class="small">LCIPHRON</span>: Not certainly identified, although the context makes it clear that he must be a contemporary of Marcus’s. He might be the Alciphron who authored a surviving collection of imaginary letters from courtesans, fishermen, etc., or a philosopher from Magnesia on the Maeander, quoted twice by the third-century antiquarian Athenaeus. (10.31)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> A<span class="small">LEXANDER</span> (1) “<span class="small">THE</span> L<span class="small">ITERARY</span> C<span class="small">RITIC</span>”: A Greek from Cotiaeum in Syria, teacher of the great orator Aelius Aristides, as well as Marcus. (1.10)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> A<span class="small">LEXANDER</span> (2) “<span class="small">THE</span> P<span class="small">LATONIST</span>”: A literary figure, mockingly dubbed Alexander Peloplaton (“The Play-Doh Plato”) by his rivals. He served as head of the Greek side of the imperial secretariat. (1.12)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> A<span class="small">LEXANDER</span> (3) “<span class="small">THE</span> G<span class="small">REAT</span>”: (356–323 <span class="small">B.C.</span>), ruler of Macedon (336–323) who conquered much of the Near and Middle East before dying at the age of thirty-three. His career was a favorite topic for moralizers and rhetoricians. (3.3, 6.24, 8.3, 9.29, 10.27)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> A<span class="small">NTISTHENES:</span> Follower of S<span class="small">OCRATES</span> and forerunner of the Cynic school (quoted 7.36).</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> A<span class="small">NTONINUS:</span> Titus Aurelius Antoninus Pius, Roman emperor (138–161). He adopted Marcus in 138 at the age of sixteen (1.16, 1.17, 4.33, 6.30, 8.25, 9.21, 10.27). Marcus also refers to himself by this name (6.44).</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> A<span class="small">POLLONIUS:</span> Apollonius of Chalcedon, Stoic philosopher and one of Marcus’s teachers. (1.8, 1.17)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> A<span class="small">RCHIMEDES:</span> Mathematician, scientist and engineer (c. 287–212 <span class="small">B.C.</span>) from the Greek city of Syracuse in Sicily, known especially for his work on hydrostatics. (6.47)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> A<span class="small">REIUS:</span> Stoic philosopher prominent at the court of A<span class="small">UGUSTUS.</span> (8.31)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> A<span class="small">RISTOPHANES:</span> Athenian comic playwright (c. 455–c. 386 <span class="small">B.C.</span>). Eleven of his approximately forty comedies survive, and are characterized by fantastic plots, scatological dialogue, outrageous political satire, and elegant choral songs. (quoted 4.23, 7.66)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> A<span class="small">SCLEPIUS:</span> Greek god of medicine. (6.43; compare 5.8 and note)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> A<span class="small">THENODOTUS:</span> A Stoic philosopher and teacher of F<span class="small">RONTO.</span> (1.13)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> A<span class="small">UGUSTUS:</span> (63 <span class="small">B.C.–A.D.</span> 14). Born Gaius Octaviaus, great-nephew and adopted son of Julius C<span class="small">AESAR.</span> He attained power following Caesar’s assassination and became sole ruler of the Roman world after defeating Caesar’s lieutenant Marcus Antonius at the battle of Actium in 31 <span class="small">B.C.</span> Through his lieutenants A<span class="small">GRIPPA</span> and M<span class="small">AECENAS</span> he was responsible for major civic improvements and an active program of literary and artistic patronage. (4.33, 8.5, 8.31)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> B<span class="small">ACCHEIUS:</span> Platonic philosopher. (1.6)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> B<span class="small">ENEDICTA:</span> Unknown, but she and T<span class="small">HEODOTUS</span> were most likely household slaves. (1.17)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> B<span class="small">RUTUS:</span> Marcus Junius Brutus (85–42 <span class="small">B.C.</span>), Roman aristocrat and politician who led the conspiracy to assassinate Julius C<span class="small">AESAR</span> in 44 <span class="small">B.C.</span> and committed suicide when the battle of Philippi ended hopes of restoring the Republic. (1.14)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> C<span class="small">AEDICIANUS:</span> Perhaps identical with a governor of Dacia in the 120s and 130s. (4.50)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> C<span class="small">AESAR:</span> Gaius Julius Caesar (100–44 <span class="small">B.C.</span>), Roman politician and general who marched on Rome in 49 <span class="small">B.C.,</span> precipitating a civil war against forces loyal to P<span class="small">OMPEY</span> and the Senate. After the defeat of the Republican forces at the battle of Pharsalia and the murder of Pompey he was made dictator for life, but assassinated in 44 <span class="small">B.C.</span> (3.3, 8.3)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> C<span class="small">AESO:</span> Unknown, though obviously a figure from Republican history. (4.33)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> C<span class="small">AMILLUS:</span> Marcus Furius Camillus, the (perhaps mythical) fourth-century <span class="small">B.C.</span> general who saved Rome when it was under attack by invading Gauls. (4.33)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> C<span class="small">ATO</span> (1): Marcus Porcius Cato “the Elder,” consul and censor in the second century <span class="small">B.C.;</span> author of a surviving work on agriculture and a lost history. He was an emblem of Roman moral rectitude and rough virtue. (4.33)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> C<span class="small">ATO</span> (2): Marcus Porcius Cato “the Younger” (95–46 <span class="small">B.C.</span>), great-grandson of Cato (1), a senator and well-known Stoic in the late Republic. He fought on the Republican side against Julius C<span class="small">AESAR</span> and committed suicide after the battle of Thapsus. He was immortalized in the poet Lucan’s epic *The Civil War,* and became an emblem of Stoic resistance to tyranny. (1.14)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> C<span class="small">ATULUS:</span> Cinna Catulus is named, along with M<span class="small">AXIMUS</span>, as a Stoic mentor of Marcus’s by the *Historia Augusta,* but nothing else is known of him. (1.13)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> C<span class="small">ECROPS:</span> Legendary founder of Athens. (4.23)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> C<span class="small">ELER:</span> Rhetorician who taught both Marcus and Lucius V<span class="small">ERUS.</span> (8.25)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> C<span class="small">HABRIAS:</span> Evidently an associate of H<span class="small">ADRIAN</span> (2), like D<span class="small">IOTIMUS</span>, but not otherwise known. (8.37)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> C<span class="small">HARAX:</span> Perhaps Charax of Pergamum, a historian known from other sources to have been active in the second or third century. (8.25)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> C<span class="small">HRYSIPPUS:</span> Stoic philosopher (280–207 <span class="small">B.C.</span>), succeeded Zeno and Cleanthes as leader of the school. His writings laid out the fundamental doctrines of early Stoicism. (6.42, 7.19)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> C<span class="small">LOTHO:</span> One of the three Fates of Greek mythology who are imagined as spinning or weaving human fortunes. (4.34)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> C<span class="small">RATES:</span> Cynic philosopher (c. 365–285 <span class="small">B.C.</span>) and disciple of D<span class="small">IOGENES.</span> (6.13)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> C<span class="small">RITO:</span> Most likely the physician Titus Statilius Crito, active under Trajan. (10.31)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> C<span class="small">ROESUS:</span> Sixth-century king of Lydia, famous for his wealth and power until his kingdom fell to the Persians. (10.27)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> D<span class="small">EMETER:</span> Greek goddess of agriculture. (6.43)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> D<span class="small">EMETRIUS</span> (1) <span class="small">OF</span> P<span class="small">HALERUM:</span> Fourth-century <span class="small">B.C.</span> philosopher, student of T<span class="small">HEOPHRASTUS</span> and governor of Athens under Macedonian rule. (9.29)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> D<span class="small">EMETRIUS</span> (2) <span class="small">THE</span> P<span class="small">LATONIST:</span> Probably not Demetrius (1), who was an adherent of the Peripatetic school, not a Platonist. A Cynic philosopher banished by V<span class="small">ESPASIAN</span> has also been suggested, but the reference is more likely to a contemporary figure now unknown. (8.25)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> D<span class="small">EMOCRITUS:</span> Pre-Socratic philosopher (c. 460–370 <span class="small">B.C.</span>) best known for developing the theory of atoms later adopted by the Epicureans. (3.3; quoted 4.3, 4.24, 7.31a)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> D<span class="small">ENTATUS:</span> Manius Curius Dentatus, third-century <span class="small">B.C.</span> Roman general. (4.33)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> D<span class="small">IOGENES:</span> Greek philosopher (c. 400–c. 325 <span class="small">B.C.</span>) and founder of the Cynic school, notable for his extreme ascetic lifestyle and contempt for social conventions. (8.3, 11.6)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> D<span class="small">IOGNETUS:</span> Marcus’s drawing teacher (according to the *Historia Augusta*), though the entry suggests that he played a greater role in Marcus’s development than this might suggest. (1.6)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> D<span class="small">ION:</span> Sicilian aristocrat, a protégé of Plato, who saw in him a potential philosopher-king. (1.14)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> D<span class="small">IOTIMUS:</span> Evidently an associate of H<span class="small">ADRIAN</span> (2), not otherwise known. (8.25, 8.37)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> D<span class="small">OMITIUS:</span> Unidentified, perhaps a student of A<span class="small">THENODOTUS.</span> (1.13)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> E<span class="small">MPEDOCLES:</span> Fifth-century <span class="small">B.C.</span> Greek philosopher and poet who regarded the natural world as the result of constant mingling and separating of four basic elements. (quoted 8.41, 12.3)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> E<span class="small">PICTETUS:</span> Stoic philosopher (c. 55–c. 135), a former slave from Phrygia who was among the most influential figures in later Stoicism. A record of his lectures and discussions (the *Discourses*) was published by his student Arrian, along with an abridged version (the *Encheiridion,* or “Handbook”). See also Introduction. (1.7, 7.19; quoted or paraphrased 4.41, 5.29, 7.63, 11.33–34, 11.36–38; cf. 4.49a and note)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> E<span class="small">PICURUS:</span> Greek philosopher (341–270 <span class="small">B.C.</span>) and founder of one of the two great Hellenistic philosophical systems. Epicureans identified pleasure as the supreme good in life and viewed the world as a random conglomeration of atoms, not ruled by any larger providence. (quoted 7.64, 9.41; compare 11.26)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> E<span class="small">PITYNCHANUS:</span> Perhaps a slave or freedman of H<span class="small">ADRIAN</span> (2). (8.25)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> E<span class="small">UDAEMON:</span> Perhaps to be identified with a literary official prominent under Hadrian (2). (8.25)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> E<span class="small">UDOXUS:</span> Greek mathematician and astronomer active in the fourth century <span class="small">B.C.</span> (6.47)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> E<span class="small">UPHRATES:</span> Perhaps the philosopher mentioned by Pliny the Younger (*Letters* 1.10) and evidently close to H<span class="small">ADRIAN</span> (2), but he might be a later imperial official mentioned by Galen. (10.31)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> E<span class="small">URIPIDES:</span> Athenian playwright (480s–407/6 <span class="small">B.C.</span>); some twenty of his tragedies are still extant. His plays were controversial in his lifetime, but in subsequent centuries he was among the most popular of Greek authors, thanks in large part to his quotability and accessible style. (quoted 7.38, 7.40–42, 7.50–51, 11.6)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> E<span class="small">UTYCHES:</span> Unknown; the comparison with S<span class="small">ATYRON</span> does not help us identify him. (10.31)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> E<span class="small">UTYCHION:</span> Not certainly identified, unless the name is a slip for the grammarian Eutychius Proculus. (10.31)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> F<span class="small">ABIUS:</span> Unidentified, perhaps identical with F<span class="small">ABIUS</span> C<span class="small">ATULLINUS.</span> (4.50)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> F<span class="small">ABIUS</span> C<span class="small">ATULLINUS:</span> Unknown. Perhaps to be identified with the F<span class="small">ABIUS</span> of 4.50. (12.27)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> F<span class="small">AUSTINA:</span> Wife of A<span class="small">NTONINUS</span> Pius (8.25). Marcus married their daughter, also Faustina (1.17).</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> F<span class="small">RONTO:</span> Marcus Cornelius Fronto (c. 95–c. 166), rhetorician from Cirta in North Africa, and a key figure in Marcus’s education. Portions of his letters to Marcus survive in two palimpsest manuscripts discovered in the early nineteenth century. (1.11)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> H<span class="small">ADRIAN</span> (1): Prominent rhetorician; no relation to the emperor. (8.25)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> H<span class="small">ADRIAN</span> (2): Roman emperor (117–138), best known for his travels and cultural interests; adopted A<span class="small">NTONINUS</span> as his heir on the condition that the latter adopt Marcus and Lucius V<span class="small">ERUS.</span> (4.33, 8.5, 8.37, 10.27)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> H<span class="small">ELVIDIUS:</span> Helvidius Priscus (died c. 75), son-in-law of T<span class="small">HRASEA</span> Paetus, exiled and later executed for his opposition to the emperor V<span class="small">ESPASIAN.</span> (1.14)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> H<span class="small">ERACLITUS:</span> Pre-Socratic philosopher (active c. 500 <span class="small">B.C.</span>) from the city of Ephesus, famous for his cryptic and paradoxical utterances. His exaltation of the *logos* as a cosmic power and his identification of fire as the primal substance were important influences on the Stoics (see also Introduction). According to the third-century <span class="small">A.D.</span> biographer Diogenes Laertius, he died of dropsy, which he tried to cure by immersing himself in manure; this account is almost certainly a later fiction. (3.3, 6.47, 8.3; quoted or paraphrased 4.46, 6.42)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> H<span class="small">IPPARCHUS:</span> Second-century <span class="small">B.C.</span> Greek astronomer. (6.47)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> H<span class="small">IPPOCRATES:</span> Greek doctor active in the fifth century <span class="small">B.C.;</span> various medical writings are transmitted under his name, as is the Hippocratic Oath still administered to doctors. (3.3)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> H<span class="small">YMEN:</span> Unknown; the comparison with S<span class="small">ATYRON</span> does not help identify him. (10.31)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> J<span class="small">ULIAN:</span> This may be a friend of F<span class="small">RONTO</span>’s, Claudius Julianus, a proconsul of Asia at about this period. (4.50)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> L<span class="small">EPIDUS:</span> This might perhaps be the Roman aristocrat who briefly shared power with Marcus Antonius and the future emperor A<span class="small">UGUSTUS,</span> but the context suggests an older contemporary of Marcus’s. (4.50)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> L<span class="small">UCILLA:</span> Marcus’s mother (d. 155/161). (1.3, 1.17, 8.25, 9.21)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> L<span class="small">USIUS</span> L<span class="small">UPUS:</span> Unknown. (12.27)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> M<span class="small">AECENAS:</span> Adviser and unofficial minister of culture to A<span class="small">UGUSTUS;</span> patron of the poets Vergil and Horace, among others. (8.31)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> M<span class="small">ARCIANUS:</span> Unknown philosopher. (1.6)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> M<span class="small">AXIMUS:</span> Claudius Maximus. Roman consul in the early 140s. Governor of Upper Pannonia in the early 150s. Later in that decade he governed North Africa, where he served as judge in the trial of the novelist Apuleius for sorcery. (1.15, 1.16, 1.17, 8.25)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> M<span class="small">ENIPPUS:</span> Cynic philosopher (early third century <span class="small">B.C.</span>) from Gadara in Syria. He features as a character in many of the satirical dialogues of Lucian. (6.47)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> M<span class="small">ONIMUS:</span> Fourth-century <span class="small">B.C.</span> Cynic philosopher and student of D<span class="small">IOGENES</span>. (2.15)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> N<span class="small">ERO:</span> Roman emperor (54–68); his name was a byword for tyranny and cruelty. (3.16)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> O<span class="small">RIGANION:</span> Unknown; most likely an imperial slave or freedman. (6.47)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> P<span class="small">ANTHEIA:</span> Mistress of Lucius V<span class="small">ERUS,</span> mentioned in several works by the satirist Lucian. (8.37)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> P<span class="small">ERDICCAS:</span> King of Macedon (c. 450–413 <span class="small">B.C.</span>). (11.25)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> P<span class="small">ERGAMOS:</span> Evidently an associate of Lucius V<span class="small">ERUS,</span> perhaps a slave or lover. (8.37)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> P<span class="small">HALARIS:</span> Sixth-century <span class="small">B.C.</span> dictator of Agrigento in Sicily, notorious for his cruelty. (3.16)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> P<span class="small">HILIP:</span> King of Macedon (359–336 <span class="small">B.C.</span>) and father of A<span class="small">LEXANDER THE</span> G<span class="small">REAT.</span> (9.29, 10.27)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> P<span class="small">HILISTION:</span> Unknown, most likely an imperial slave or freedman, though a contemporary mime writer of this name is also known. (6.47)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> P<span class="small">HOCION:</span> Athenian general and statesman of the fourth century <span class="small">B.C.</span> He was eventually sentenced to death for treason, and before his execution supposedly asked his son to forgive the Athenians for condemning him. (11.13)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> P<span class="small">HOEBUS:</span> Unknown, most likely an imperial slave or freedman. (6.47)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> P<span class="small">LATO:</span> Athenian philosopher (c. 429–347 <span class="small">B.C.</span>), disciple of S<span class="small">OCRATES</span> and author of philosophical dialogues in which the latter is portrayed debating with his disciples and other contemporary figures. The most famous of these is perhaps the *Republic,* in which he envisions an ideal society. (7.48, 9.29, 10.23; quoted 7.44–46)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> P<span class="small">OMPEY:</span> Gnaeus Pompeius Magnus (106–48 <span class="small">B.C.</span>), Roman politician and general who rose to power in the 60s on the basis of a series of successful campaigns in the East. His brief political alliance with Julius C<span class="small">AESAR</span> gave way to mutual rivalry and suspicion. When Caesar’s march on Rome precipitated civil war in 49, Pompey led the senatorial resistance. Following his defeat at the battle of Pharsalus, he fled to Egypt, where he was murdered. (3.3, 8.3; family 8.31)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> P<span class="small">YTHAGORAS:</span> Greek mathematician, philosopher, and mystic of the late sixth century <span class="small">B.C.</span> He founded a religious community in southern Italy whose members were known especially for their devotion to music and geometry. (6.47; compare 11.27)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> R<span class="small">USTICUS:</span> Quintus Junius Rusticus, twice consul and city prefect of Rome in the mid-160s. His influence on Marcus is attested by the *Historia Augusta,* although the reference to him in 1.17 suggests that their relationship had its ups and downs. (1.7, 1.17)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> S<span class="small">ATYRON:</span> Unknown, though evidently a contemporary of Marcus. (10.31)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> S<span class="small">CIPIO:</span> Either Publius Cornelius Scipio Africanus (c. 235–183 <span class="small">B.C.</span>), who defeated Hannibal in the Second Punic War, or his grandson by adoption, Publius Cornelius Scipio Aemilianus (185/4–129 <span class="small">B.C.</span>), the conqueror of Carthage in the Third Punic War. (4.33)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> S<span class="small">ECUNDA:</span> Wife of M<span class="small">AXIMUS</span>. (8.25)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> S<span class="small">EVERUS</span> (1): Lucius Catilius Severus, Marcus’s great-grandfather. (1.4)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> S<span class="small">EVERUS</span> (2): Gnaeus Claudius Severus Arabianus from Pompeiopolis in Asia Minor, consul in 146; his son (perhaps the Severus of 10.31) married one of Marcus’s daughters. He was an adherent of the Peripatetic school, which traced its heritage back to Aristotle. (1.14)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> S<span class="small">EXTUS:</span> Sextus of Chaeronea, Stoic philosopher, teacher of both Marcus and Lucius V<span class="small">ERUS,</span> and nephew of the great biographer and antiquarian Plutarch. (1.9)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> S<span class="small">ILVANUS:</span> Perhaps Lamia Silvanus, a son-in-law of Marcus. (10.31)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> S<span class="small">OCRATES:</span> Athenian philosopher (469–399 <span class="small">B.C.</span>), teacher of P<span class="small">LATO.</span> <span class="small"></span> He spent most of his life in his native city, and served with distinction in the Peloponnesian War against Sparta. Although associated with several members of the aristocratic junta that ruled Athens after its defeat in 404, he refused to participate in their atrocities. He was executed by the Athenians on a charge of impiety following the restoration of democracy; Plato’s *Apology* purports to give his speech at the trial. (1.16, 3.3, 3.6, 6.47, 7.19, 7.66, 8.3, 11.23, 11.25, 11.28, 11.39)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> S<span class="small">OCRATICUS:</span> Unknown; the comparison with S<span class="small">ATYRON</span> does not help identify him. (10.31)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> S<span class="small">TERTINIUS:</span> Not certainly identified. Tacitus mentions an army officer of this name in the reign of Tiberius. But the reference to Baiae (a Roman resort on the Bay of Naples) suggests a more likely candidate a generation or so later: the wealthy Neapolitan physician Quintus Stertinius, mentioned by Pliny the Elder (*Natural History* 29.7). (12.27)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> T<span class="small">ANDASIS:</span> Philosopher mentioned along with one Marcianus; neither is otherwise known. Some have suggested a scribe’s error for Basilides, listed among Marcus’s teachers by other sources. (1.6)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> T<span class="small">ELAUGES:</span> Apparently a lesser disciple of S<span class="small">OCRATES,</span> unless the reference is to the son of P<span class="small">YTHAGORAS</span> by this name. (7.66)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> T<span class="small">HEODOTUS:</span> Unknown, but he and B<span class="small">ENEDICTA</span> were most likely household slaves. (1.17)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> T<span class="small">HEOPHRASTUS:</span> Philosopher (c. 371–c. 287 <span class="small">B.C.</span>) who succeeded Aristotle as head of the Peripatetic school. (2.10)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> T<span class="small">HRASEA:</span> Publius Clodius Thrasea Paetus (d. 66), Roman aristocrat (consul 56) and father-in-law of H<span class="small">ELVIDIUS</span> Priscus. His opposition to the regime of N<span class="small">ERO</span> (by whom he was eventually forced to commit suicide) was informed by Stoic philosophy and in particular by the example of the younger C<span class="small">ATO</span> (2), of whom he wrote a biography. (1.14)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> T<span class="small">IBERIUS:</span> Roman emperor (14–37) who succeeded A<span class="small">UGUSTUS</span>. Late in his reign he withdrew to a private estate on the island of Capri; his alleged excesses there are recorded in the biography of him by Suetonius. (12.27)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> T<span class="small">RAJAN:</span> Marcus Ulpius Traianus, Roman general and emperor (98–117). (4.32)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> T<span class="small">ROPAEOPHORUS:</span> Perhaps a contemporary senator named in an inscription from Perinthus. (10.31)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> V<span class="small">ELIUS</span> R<span class="small">UFUS:</span> Addressee of one of F<span class="small">RONTO</span>’s letters, but otherwise unknown. (12.27)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> V<span class="small">ERUS</span> (1): Marcus Annius Verus (d. 138), grandfather of Marcus. He was three times consul (the last two in 121 and 126); he also served as city prefect of Rome about this time. After the death of his wife he evidently took a concubine who helped raise Marcus. (1.1, 1.17, 9.21)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> V<span class="small">ERUS</span> (2): Marcus Annius Verus, father of Marcus and husband of L<span class="small">UCILLA.</span> He died sometime between 130 and 135. (1.2, 8.25)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> V<span class="small">ERUS</span> (3): Lucius Aurelius Verus (130–169), son of H<span class="small">ADRIAN</span> (2)’s intended successor, Lucius Aelius. Originally named Lucius Ceionius Commodus, he was adopted along with Marcus by Antoninus Pius and on Antoninus’s death became co-emperor with Marcus. He was entrusted with the conduct of the Parthian War, and campaigned with Marcus on the northern frontier before his sudden death on the way back to Rome. (1.17, 8.37)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> V<span class="small">ESPASIAN:</span> Roman emperor (69–79). His reign represented a period of stability after the power struggle that followed the death of N<span class="small">ERO</span>, but he came into conflict with some members of the senatorial class, notably the Stoic H<span class="small">ELVIDIUS</span> Priscus. (4.32)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> V<span class="small">OLESUS:</span> Traditional surname in the Valerius clan, which produced a number of figures prominent in early historical accounts. Which one Marcus has in mind is uncertain. (4.33)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> X<span class="small">ANTHIPPE:</span> Wife of S<span class="small">OCRATES</span> and proverbially a shrew. (11.28)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> X<span class="small">ENOCRATES:</span> Platonic philosopher and head of the Academy at the end of the fourth century <span class="small">B.C.</span> (6.13)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> X<span class="small">ENOPHON:</span> Probably a contemporary doctor mentioned by Galen. (10.31)</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_11">

<span class="calibre6"> Z<span class="small">EUS:</span> Sky god and head of the Greek pantheon; Marcus refers to him only rarely and normally prefers a vaguer formulation such as “God” or “the gods.” (4.23, 5.7, 5.8, 11.8)</span>

</div>

<div id="index_split_031.html_calibre_pb_43" class="mbp_pagebreak">

</div>

<span id="index_split_032.html"></span>

<div id="index_split_032.html_filepos528958" class="calibre_3">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_2">

<span class="calibre6"> A<span class="small">BOUT THE</span> T<span class="small">RANSLATOR</span></span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_8">

<span class="calibre4"> G<span class="small">REGORY</span> H<span class="small">AYS</span> is assistant professor of classics at the University of Virginia. He has published articles and reviews on various ancient writers and is currently completing a translation and critical study of the mythographer Fulgentius. </span>

</div>

<div id="index_split_032.html_calibre_pb_44" class="mbp_pagebreak">

</div>

<span id="index_split_033.html"></span>

<div id="index_split_033.html_filepos529487" class="calibre_3">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_2">

<span class="calibre6"> T<span class="small">HE</span> M<span class="small">ODERN</span> L<span class="small">IBRARY</span> E<span class="small">DITORIAL</span> B<span class="small">OARD</span></span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_2">

<span class="calibre6"> Maya Angelou</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_2">

<span class="calibre6"> Daniel J. Boorstin</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_2">

<span class="calibre6"> A. S. Byatt</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_2">

<span class="calibre6"> Caleb Carr</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_2">

<span class="calibre6"> Christopher Cerf</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_2">

<span class="calibre6"> Ron Chernow</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_2">

<span class="calibre6"> Shelby Foote</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_2">

<span class="calibre6"> Stephen Jay Gould</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_2">

<span class="calibre6"> Vartan Gregorian</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_2">

<span class="calibre6"> Richard Howard</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_2">

<span class="calibre6"> Charles Johnson</span>

</div>

<div class="calibre_5">

 

</div>

<div class="calibre_2">
